The holonomy map

From the developments in the partially hyperbolicity theory by Brin-Pesin [13] and Pugh-Shub [32] we have that the projection along leaves, also known as holonomies, between pair of transversal surfaces to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{ss}$$\end{document}Fss have a Hölder continuous Jacobian with respect to Lebesgue measure. This implies a similar conclusion for the holonomies transversal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{cs}$$\end{document}Fcs. It follows that the holonomy between pairs of transversal curves to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}_\Sigma ^{ss}$$\end{document}FΣss along the lines of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{ss}_\Sigma $$\end{document}FΣss can be thought of as interval maps having a Hölder Jacobian. Hence these holonomies are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1+\alpha }$$\end{document}C1+α for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha <1$$\end{document}0<α<1. In this setting, the leaves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^ss(x,\Sigma )$$\end{document}Wss(x,Σ), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \Sigma $$\end{document}x∈Σ, determine a foliation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{ss}_\Sigma $$\end{document}FΣss of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma $$\end{document}Σ with transversal smoothness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1+\alpha }$$\end{document}C1+α.

Therefore, we can assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma $$\end{document}Σ is the image of the unit square \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\times I$$\end{document}I×I under the action of a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1+\alpha }$$\end{document}C1+α diffeomorphism h, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha <1$$\end{document}0<α<1. Furthermore, h sends vertical lines inside the leaves of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{ss}_\Sigma $$\end{document}FΣss. The next step is to prove that the strong stable foliation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{ss}(x)$$\end{document}Fss(x) is not only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1}$$\end{document}C1 but also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1+\alpha }$$\end{document}C1+α, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha <1$$\end{document}0<α<1. This was done by Araújo, Melbourne and Varandas in [7], stated as Lemma 2.2. This result is a consequence of domination of the splitting (Equation (19)), uniform contraction along the stable direction (Equation (20)) and strong dissipativity (Definition 3.3). Therefore, we can conclude that the neutral geometrical flow has a strong-stable foliation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{ss}(x)$$\end{document}Fss(x) which is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1+\alpha }$$\end{document}C1+α. Furthermore, the modified return map also has a strong-stable foliation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{ss}_\Sigma $$\end{document}FΣss, whose transversal smoothness is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1+\alpha }$$\end{document}C1+α, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha <1$$\end{document}0<α<1.

A final remark concerning this subsection, Theorem 6 in [6] is stated for a singular hyperbolic attractor. However, the conclusions and arguments still hold true if we consider a compact partially hyperbolic invariant set instead of a singular hyperbolic set. We will also like to mention that the situation for the origin is slightly different, since the splitting of the tangent bundle is given by a one-dimensional strong-stable direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^s$$\end{document}Es and a two-dimensional center direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^c$$\end{document}Ec. However, we are only concerned with the existence of the strong-stable manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{ss}({\bar{0}})$$\end{document}Wss(0¯), which follows from the theory of partial hyperbolicity.

The UNI Condition

The main goal of this subsection is to show that the stable and unstable manifolds of the modified geometrical model are jointly nonintegrable. The joint nonintegrability of stable and unstable foliations can be interpreted as follows: The stable and unstable foliation of an Anosov flow are always transversal, therefore, if they are jointly integrable, this provides us with a codimension one invariant foliation which is transversal to the flow direction. In contrast, if there exists a codimension one submanifold transversal to the flow direction, then this foliation must be subfoliated by both the stable and unstable foliations. Thus they must be jointly integrable. In this situation it is known [23, Proposition 3.3] that the flow is semiconjugate to a suspension with a locally constant roof-function over a subshift of finite type. Such a flow need not mix! From the work of Araújo, Butterley and Varandas [8], we know that the joint nonintegrability of the stable and unstable manifolds is equivalent to the uniformly nonintegrability (UNI) condition. As we will see later in this section, the UNI condition, stated formally in Definition 3.7, will ensure that the roof function of the suspension flow, which we will use to model the Neutral geometrical Lorenz flow, is not cohomologous to a constant function; that is, the time it takes for a point in the base to reach the roof of the suspension flow is not the same for every point. This property will guarantee the mixing properties.

In this chapter we will aim to prove the UNI condition. In [5] and [7] Araújo et al. prove the UNI condition by exploiting the properties obtained by using hyperbolic times. From now on, when we talk about the neutral geometrical model, we will refer to both models we constructed in Sect. 2.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I=[-1,1]$$\end{document}I=[-1,1] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{{\text {Neu}}} : I \rightarrow I$$\end{document}fNeu:I→I be the one-dimensional Lorenz-like map obtained from the neutral geometrical model. We notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{0\}$$\end{document}{0} is a nondegenerate2 exceptional set for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}fNeu. From Theorem 4.3 in [5] we know that there are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Observation 3.5

The tails of the return time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ and its extension also denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ are exponential (see [33]); Now, we construct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F : X \rightarrow X$$\end{document}F:X→X the Poincaré map by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(x)=P_{{\text {Neu}}}^{\tau (\pi (x))}(x)$$\end{document}F(x)=PNeuτ(π(x))(x) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in X$$\end{document}x∈X. Furthermore, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Q}$$\end{document}Q be the measurable partition of X by taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcup \{W^{ss}_{P_{{\text {Neu}}}}(x)\;|\;x\in {\tilde{Q}}\}$$\end{document}⋃{WPNeuss(x)|x∈Q~} as its elements, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{Q}}\in \tilde{\mathcal {Q}}$$\end{document}Q~∈Q~. We will make use of X and F when making the model of the neutral geometrical Lorenz flow by a suspension flow.

We take the induced roof function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R : X \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}R:X→R+ given by22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R(x)=\sum _{k=0}^{\tau (x)-1}r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^k(x)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R(x)=∑k=0τ(x)-1rNeu(PNeuk(x)).Notice that R is constant along stable leaves because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {neu}}}$$\end{document}rneu is constants along stable leaves. With this in mind we can state the definition of the UNI condition. First, we give the definition of suspension flow.

Definition 3.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Sigma ,\nu )$$\end{document}(Σ,ν) be a probability space and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P : \Sigma \rightarrow \Sigma $$\end{document}P:Σ→Σ an ergodic measure-preserving transformation. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r : \Sigma \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}r:Σ→R+ be a measurable (Hölder continuous) roof function. We define the suspension space as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma ^r=\{(x,u)\in \Sigma \times [0,r(x)]\}/\sim $$\end{document}Σr={(x,u)∈Σ×[0,r(x)]}/∼, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,r(x))\sim (P(x),0)$$\end{document}(x,r(x))∼(P(x),0). The suspension flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_t : \Sigma ^r \rightarrow \Sigma ^r$$\end{document}ft:Σr→Σr is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_t(x,u)=(x,u+t)$$\end{document}ft(x,u)=(x,u+t) computed modulo identifications and the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =\nu \times \lambda $$\end{document}μ=ν×λ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ is the Lebesgue measure, is ergodic and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_t$$\end{document}ft-invariant. In the finite measure case, we normalise by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{r}}=\int _\Sigma rd\mu $$\end{document}r¯=∫Σrdμ so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =\frac{\nu \times \lambda }{{\bar{r}}}$$\end{document}μ=ν×λr¯ is a probability measure.

Definition 3.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R : X \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}R:X→R+ be a roof function as above, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t : X^R \rightarrow X^R$$\end{document}Ft:XR→XR the suspension flow built over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F : X \rightarrow X$$\end{document}F:X→X, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_n(x)=\sum _{i=0}^{n-1}R\circ F^i(x)$$\end{document}Rn(x)=∑i=0n-1R∘Fi(x). Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{h_1,h_2}=R_n\circ h_1-R_n\circ h_2 : X \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ψh1,h2=Rn∘h1-Rn∘h2:X→R, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1,\;h_2\in \mathcal {H}_n$$\end{document}h1,h2∈Hn; that is, inverse branches of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^n$$\end{document}Fn. Then the UNI condition holds if there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D>0$$\end{document}D>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1,\;h_2\in \mathcal {H}_{n_0}$$\end{document}h1,h2∈Hn0 for some sufficiently large \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0\ge 1$$\end{document}n0≥1, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi '_{h_1,h_2}|\ge D$$\end{document}|ψh1,h2′|≥D.

We saw in the previous subsection that the Poincaré map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{{\text {Neu}}} : \Sigma \rightarrow \Sigma $$\end{document}PNeu:Σ→Σ has a strong stable foliation. We observe that the leaves of this foliation cross \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma $$\end{document}Σ, hence the induced map F has a strong stable manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{ss}_F(x)=W^{ss}_{P_{{\text {Neu}}}}(x)$$\end{document}WFss(x)=WPNeuss(x) that crosses X. Araújo et al. in [7, Proposition 2.4] provide us with local unstable manifolds of uniform size for F and defined almost everywhere, and by [7, Proposition 2.4] we obtain a local product structure, see Fig. 6.Fig. 6Local product structure for F

Local product structure for F

To use the arguments given in [7] for the temporal distortion function we need to adapt the proofs of the uniform bound of the derivative of the induced roof function R and a bound on the flow time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}rNeu. An adjustment is required because we are changing the flow time from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma $$\end{document}Σ to S. Recall that the original flow time for the geometrical Lorenz model is given by (3), whereas the modified flow time is given by (6); that is, we change from logarithmic to polynomial. More precisely, we have the following propositions.

Proposition 3.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{Q}}\in \tilde{\mathcal {Q}}$$\end{document}Q~∈Q~ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}} : {\tilde{Q}} \rightarrow {\tilde{X}}$$\end{document}F~:Q~→X~ as above. Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H the set of all inverse branches of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}}$$\end{document}F~. Then we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{h\in \mathcal {H}}\sup _{x\in {\tilde{X}}}|D(R\circ h)(x)|<\infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}suph∈Hsupx∈X~|D(R∘h)(x)|<∞.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{Q}}\in \tilde{\mathcal {Q}}$$\end{document}Q~∈Q~ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \mathcal {H}$$\end{document}h∈H; that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h : {\tilde{X}} \rightarrow {\tilde{Q}}$$\end{document}h:X~→Q~ be an inverse branch of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}}$$\end{document}F~ with inducing time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =\tau ({\tilde{Q}})\ge 1$$\end{document}τ=τ(Q~)≥1 and fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in {\tilde{Q}}$$\end{document}x∈Q~. We first observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |D(R\circ h)(x)|= & {} |DR(h(x))|\cdot |Dh(x)|=\frac{|DR(h(x))|}{|D{\tilde{F}}(h(x))|}\\= & {} \left| \sum _{i=0}^{\tau -1}\frac{(Dr_{{\text {Neu}}}\circ f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i)\cdot Df_{{\text {Neu}}}^i}{D{\tilde{F}}}\circ h(x)\right| . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|D(R∘h)(x)|=|DR(h(x))|·|Dh(x)|=|DR(h(x))||DF~(h(x))|=∑i=0τ-1(DrNeu∘fNeui)·DfNeuiDF~∘h(x).From the construction of the inducing partition using hyperbolic times (for more details on hyperbolic times see [2] and [5]), we have that backward contraction and slow recurrence to the singular point, see Observation 3.4; that is, there are constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in (0,1)$$\end{document}σ∈(0,1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0>0$$\end{document}b0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \in (0,\frac{1}{2}]$$\end{document}c∈(0,12] such that (Backward contraction) For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in {\tilde{Q}}(x)$$\end{document}y∈Q~(x)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(y)-f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x)|\le b_0\sigma ^{\tau -i}|{\tilde{F}}(y)-{\tilde{F}}(x)|, \;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fNeui(y)-fNeui(x)|≤b0στ-i|F~(y)-F~(x)|,i=0,…,τ-1.(Slow recurrence to the singular point) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x)|\ge \sigma ^{c(\tau -i)}, \;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fNeui(x)|≥σc(τ-i),i=0,…,τ-1.Notice that23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| \frac{Df_{{\text {Neu}}}^i}{D{\tilde{F}}}\circ h(x)\right| \le b_0\sigma ^{\tau -i}\;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DfNeuiDF~∘h(x)≤b0στ-ii=0,…,τ-1,where the inequality follows from the backward contraction.

(Backward contraction) For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in {\tilde{Q}}(x)$$\end{document}y∈Q~(x)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(y)-f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x)|\le b_0\sigma ^{\tau -i}|{\tilde{F}}(y)-{\tilde{F}}(x)|, \;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fNeui(y)-fNeui(x)|≤b0στ-i|F~(y)-F~(x)|,i=0,…,τ-1.

(Backward contraction) For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in {\tilde{Q}}(x)$$\end{document}y∈Q~(x)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(y)-f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x)|\le b_0\sigma ^{\tau -i}|{\tilde{F}}(y)-{\tilde{F}}(x)|, \;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fNeui(y)-fNeui(x)|≤b0στ-i|F~(y)-F~(x)|,i=0,…,τ-1.

(Slow recurrence to the singular point) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x)|\ge \sigma ^{c(\tau -i)}, \;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fNeui(x)|≥σc(τ-i),i=0,…,τ-1.

(Slow recurrence to the singular point) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x)|\ge \sigma ^{c(\tau -i)}, \;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fNeui(x)|≥σc(τ-i),i=0,…,τ-1.

Moreover, from the slow recurrence to the singularity we also get the following inequality;24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |(Dr_{{\text {Neu}}}\circ f_{{\text {Neu}}}^i)\circ h(x)|\le b_1\sigma ^{-c(1+\frac{1}{\beta _2})(\tau -i)}\;\text { }\; i=0,\ldots ,\tau -1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|(DrNeu∘fNeui)∘h(x)|≤b1σ-c(1+1β2)(τ-i)i=0,…,τ-1,for some constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_1>0$$\end{document}b1>0. Altogether, Equations (23) and (24) imply that25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |D(R\circ h)(x)|\le b\sum _{i=0}^{\tau -1}\sigma ^{si}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|D(R∘h)(x)|≤b∑i=0τ-1σsi,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=1-c(1+\frac{1}{\beta _2})$$\end{document}s=1-c(1+1β2). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<c\le \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}0<c≤12 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _2>2$$\end{document}β2>2, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s<1$$\end{document}s<1. Therefore the sum converges, and we have that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{h\in \mathcal {H}}\sup _{x\in {\tilde{X}}}|D(R\circ h)(x)|<\infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}suph∈Hsupx∈X~|D(R∘h)(x)|<∞.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,\;y\in {\tilde{X}}$$\end{document}x,y∈X~ we define the separation time s(x, y) as the least integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 0$$\end{document}n≥0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}}^n(x)$$\end{document}F~n(x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}}^n(y)$$\end{document}F~n(y) are in different partition elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Q}_0$$\end{document}Q0. For given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\eta <1$$\end{document}0<η<1, the symbolic metric is defined on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{X}}$$\end{document}X~ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_\eta (x,y)=\eta ^{s(x,y)}$$\end{document}dη(x,y)=ηs(x,y). Finally, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {Neu}}}^{(k)}(x)=\sum _{i=0}^{k-1}r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x))$$\end{document}rNeu(k)(x)=∑i=0k-1rNeu(PNeui(x)).

Proposition 3.9

There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B>0$$\end{document}B>0 such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,\; y\in {\tilde{X}}$$\end{document}x,y∈X~ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s(x,y)\ge 1$$\end{document}s(x,y)≥1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le k\le \tau (x)=\tau (y)$$\end{document}0≤k≤τ(x)=τ(y) we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|r^{(k)}_{{\text {Neu}}}(x)-r^{(k)}_{{\text {Neu}}}(y)|\le B|{\tilde{F}}(x)-{\tilde{F}}(y)|^\epsilon $$\end{document}|rNeu(k)(x)-rNeu(k)(y)|≤B|F~(x)-F~(y)|ϵ. Consequently, there is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in (0,1)$$\end{document}η∈(0,1) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|R|_\eta <\infty $$\end{document}|R|η<∞, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|R|_\eta =\sup _{x\ne y}\frac{|R(x)-R(y)|}{d_\eta (x,y)}$$\end{document}|R|η=supx≠y|R(x)-R(y)|dη(x,y) denotes the Lipschitz constant of the quotient induced roof function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R : {\tilde{X}} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}R:X~→R+ with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_\eta $$\end{document}dη. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\tilde{F}}(x)-{\tilde{F}}(y)|\le Bd_\eta (x,y)$$\end{document}|F~(x)-F~(y)|≤Bdη(x,y).

Proof

For convenience, in this proof f will denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}fNeu. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,\;y\in {\tilde{X}}$$\end{document}x,y∈X~ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s(x,y)=n\ge 1$$\end{document}s(x,y)=n≥1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le k\le \tau (x)=\tau (y)$$\end{document}0≤k≤τ(x)=τ(y). Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in {\tilde{Q}}^n(x)$$\end{document}y∈Q~n(x), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{Q}}^n(x)=\displaystyle \bigvee _{i=0}^{n-1}({\tilde{F}}^i)^{-1}({\tilde{Q}}(x))$$\end{document}Q~n(x)=⋁i=0n-1(F~i)-1(Q~(x)) is the nth refinement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{Q}}(x)$$\end{document}Q~(x), and so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ({\tilde{F}}^i(x))=\tau ({\tilde{F}}^i(y))$$\end{document}τ(F~i(x))=τ(F~i(y)) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=0,\ldots ,n-1$$\end{document}i=0,…,n-1. Hence, the choice of the cross- section assures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}rNeu is constant along stable leaves and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {Neu}}}(x)=|x|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}h(x)+\tau _2(x)$$\end{document}rNeu(x)=|x|-1β2h(x)+τ2(x), where h(x) is bounded and bounded away from zero. In fact, h(x) is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_0 + \mathcal {O}(|x|^\gamma )$$\end{document}h0+O(|x|γ) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_0$$\end{document}h0 is a positive constant and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma > 0$$\end{document}γ>0 depending on whether the higher order terms are consider in (7) or not. Also h(x) is differentiable for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x > 0$$\end{document}x>0, because the Dulac map is differentiable. Then we can write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |R(x)-R(y)|\le & {} \sum _{i=0}^{\tau (x)-1}|r_{{\text {Neu}}}(f^i(x))-r_{{\text {Neu}}}(f^i(y))|\\\le & {} \sum _{i=0}^{\tau (x)-1}\left| |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}h(f^i(x))-|f^i(y)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}h(f^i(y))\right| \\{} & {} +|\tau _2(f^i(x)-\tau _2(f^i(y))|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|R(x)-R(y)|≤∑i=0τ(x)-1|rNeu(fi(x))-rNeu(fi(y))|≤∑i=0τ(x)-1|fi(x)|-1β2h(fi(x))-|fi(y)|-1β2h(fi(y))+|τ2(fi(x)-τ2(fi(y))|.We first notice that,26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| \tau _2(f^i(y))-\tau _2(f^i(x))\right|\le & {} \left\Vert \tau _2\right\Vert _{\epsilon }\left| f^i(y)-f^i(x)\right| ^\epsilon \nonumber \\\le & {} \sigma ^{\epsilon (\tau (x)-i)}\left\Vert \tau _2\right\Vert _{\epsilon }\left| {\tilde{F}}^i(y)-{\tilde{F}}^i(x)\right| ^\epsilon . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τ2(fi(y))-τ2(fi(x))≤τ2ϵfi(y)-fi(x)ϵ≤σϵ(τ(x)-i)τ2ϵF~i(y)-F~i(x)ϵ.The second inequality in (26) follows from Observation 3.4-1. Now, denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bigg | |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}h(f^i(x)) - |f^i(y)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}h(f^i(y))\Bigg |$$\end{document}||fi(x)|-1β2h(fi(x))-|fi(y)|-1β2h(fi(y))| by A, then we obtain that,27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A\le & {} \left| |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} - |f^i(y)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} \right| |h(f^i(x))| \nonumber \\{} & {} + |f^i(y)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} |h(f^i(x))-h(f^i(y))|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A≤|fi(x)|-1β2-|fi(y)|-1β2|h(fi(x))|+|fi(y)|-1β2|h(fi(x))-h(fi(y))|.We notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} - |f^i(y)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}\right| $$\end{document}|fi(x)|-1β2-|fi(y)|-1β2 is bounded. Indeed we have the following:28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} - |f^i(y)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}\right|= & {} |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} \left| 1 - \left( \frac{ |f^i(y)|}{|f^i(x)|} \right) ^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}\right| \nonumber \\\le & {} |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} \left| 1 - \left( 1 + \frac{ |f^i(y)-f^i(x)|}{|f^i(x)|} \right) ^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}}\right| \nonumber \\\le & {} |f^i(x)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} \frac{C_0}{\beta _2} \frac{ |f^i(y)-f^i(x)|}{|f^i(x)|} \nonumber \\= & {} \frac{C_0}{\beta _2} |f^i(x)|^{-(1+\frac{1}{\beta _2})} |f^i(y)-f^i(x)| \nonumber \\\le & {} \frac{C_0}{\beta _2} \sigma ^{\left( 1-c\left( 1+\frac{1}{\beta _2}\right) \right) (\tau (x)-i)} \left| {\tilde{F}}(y)-{\tilde{F}}(x)\right| , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fi(x)|-1β2-|fi(y)|-1β2=|fi(x)|-1β21-|fi(y)||fi(x)|-1β2≤|fi(x)|-1β21-1+|fi(y)-fi(x)||fi(x)|-1β2≤|fi(x)|-1β2C0β2|fi(y)-fi(x)||fi(x)|=C0β2|fi(x)|-(1+1β2)|fi(y)-fi(x)|≤C0β2σ1-c1+1β2(τ(x)-i)F~(y)-F~(x),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0>0$$\end{document}C0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<c\le \frac{2}{3}$$\end{document}0<c≤23. The first and second inequalities follow from the Bernoulli inequality and from Observation 3.4-1 and 2, respectively. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(f^i(x))$$\end{document}h(fi(x)) is bounded and by (28), we can bound the first term in the sum of (27) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{C_0}{\beta _2} \sigma ^{(1-c(1+\frac{1}{\beta _2}))(\tau (x)-i)}\left| {\tilde{F}}(y)- {\tilde{F}}(x)\right| $$\end{document}C0β2σ(1-c(1+1β2))(τ(x)-i)F~(y)-F~(x).

To finish the proof it remains to find a bound for29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |f^i(y)|^{-\frac{1}{\beta _2}} |h(f^i(x))-h(f^i(y))|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|fi(y)|-1β2|h(fi(x))-h(fi(y))|.Notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|h(f^i(x))-h(f^i(y))|\approx h'(\xi )|f^i(x)-f^i(y)|$$\end{document}|h(fi(x))-h(fi(y))|≈h′(ξ)|fi(x)-fi(y)|, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^i(x)<\xi <f^i(y)$$\end{document}fi(x)<ξ<fi(y). Hence, by Observations 3.4-1 and 2, we have that (29) is bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0h'(\xi )\sigma ^{(1-\frac{c}{\beta _2})(\tau (x)-i)}|{\tilde{F}}(y)-{\tilde{F}}(x)|$$\end{document}C0h′(ξ)σ(1-cβ2)(τ(x)-i)|F~(y)-F~(x)|. Assuming that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h'(\xi )$$\end{document}h′(ξ) is bounded and combining all the previous bounds we have that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |R(x)-R(y)|\le & {} C\sum _{i=0}^{\tau (x)-1}[ (\sigma ^{s(\tau (x)-i)}+\sigma ^{u(\tau (x)-i)})|{\tilde{F}}(y)-{\tilde{F}}(x)|\\{} & {} +\ \sigma ^{\epsilon (\tau (x)-i)}\left\Vert \tau _2\right\Vert _{\epsilon }|{\tilde{F}}^i(y)-{\tilde{F}}^i(x)|^\epsilon ]\\\le & {} B |{\tilde{F}}^i(y)-{\tilde{F}}^i(x)|^\epsilon , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|R(x)-R(y)|≤C∑i=0τ(x)-1[(σs(τ(x)-i)+σu(τ(x)-i))|F~(y)-F~(x)|+σϵ(τ(x)-i)τ2ϵ|F~i(y)-F~i(x)|ϵ]≤B|F~i(y)-F~i(x)|ϵ,for some constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B>0$$\end{document}B>0 where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=1-c(1+\frac{1}{\beta _2}) > 0$$\end{document}s=1-c(1+1β2)>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=1-\frac{c}{\beta _2}$$\end{document}u=1-cβ2. As in the previous proof, the sum converges since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _2>2$$\end{document}β2>2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<c\le \frac{2}{3}$$\end{document}0<c≤23 and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<s,u<1$$\end{document}0<s,u<1. This establishes what we were aiming to prove.

One caveat we have to make here is that this argument is only valid if we assume boundedness for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h'$$\end{document}h′. This is true if the higher order terms in (7) are not present. If higher order terms are present, boundedness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h'$$\end{document}h′ is plausible, but since the required perturbation argument in [17] is less constructive so as to immediately derive this boundedness, we will try to convince the reader with the numeric analysis performed at the end of this work that this boundedness is indeed true. Thus, rather than a rigorous proof we give a combination of mathematical arguments and numerical verification. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Now let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q\in \mathcal {Q}$$\end{document}Q∈Q be a partition elements for F. The temporal distortion function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T : Q\times Q \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}T:Q×Q→R is defined almost everywhere by,30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T(x,y)= & {} \sum _{i=-\infty }^\infty [r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x))-r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i([x,y]))-r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i([y,x]))\nonumber \\{} & {} +\ r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i)(y)]\nonumber \\= & {} \sum _{i=-\infty }^{-1}[r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(x))-r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i([x,y]))-r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i([y,x]))\nonumber \\{} & {} +\ r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^i(y))], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T(x,y)=∑i=-∞∞[rNeu(PNeui(x))-rNeu(PNeui([x,y]))-rNeu(PNeui([y,x]))+rNeu(PNeui)(y)]=∑i=-∞-1[rNeu(PNeui(x))-rNeu(PNeui([x,y]))-rNeu(PNeui([y,x]))+rNeu(PNeui(y))],where [x, y] is the local product of x and y (see Fig. 7). The second inequality follows from the property of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}rNeu of being constant along stable leaves.Fig. 7Local product structure for F

Local product structure for F

Now, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,\; y\in X$$\end{document}x,y∈X in the same unstable manifold for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F : X \rightarrow X$$\end{document}F:X→X we define31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_0(x,y)=\sum _{i=1}^\infty [r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^{-i}(x))- r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^{-i}(y))]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T0(x,y)=∑i=1∞[rNeu(PNeu-i(x))-rNeu(PNeu-i(y))].The continuity and other properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0$$\end{document}T0 are stated in [7, Lemma 3.1]. Furthermore, we can rewrite the temporal distortion function T(x, y) in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0$$\end{document}T0; that is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T(x,y)=T_0(x,[x,y])+T_0(y,[y,x]). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T(x,y)=T0(x,[x,y])+T0(y,[y,x]).The main result concerning the temporal distortion function establishes the joint nonintegrability of the stable and unstable foliations for the flow by proving that the temporal distortion function T is not identically zero, that is, there is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q\in \mathcal {Q}$$\end{document}Q∈Q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,\; y\in Q$$\end{document}x,y∈Q such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T(x,y)\ne 0$$\end{document}T(x,y)≠0 and is stated and proven in [7, Theorem 3.4].

We adjusted the proof of the uniform bound of the derivative of the induced roof function for the geometrical neutral Lorenz model since we want to apply [9, Proposition 7.4] in order to use the same arguments given in [3, Corollary 4.3]. Thus, we get the UNI condition for the geometrical neutral Lorenz flow. For completeness we state it in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.10

The UNI condition holds for the geometrical neutral Lorenz flow.

For fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in X$$\end{document}x∈X, we define the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h : W^{u}_F(x) \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}h:WFu(x)→R given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} h(y)=T(x,y)=T_0(x,[x,y])+T_0(y,[y,x]), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h(y)=T(x,y)=T0(x,[x,y])+T0(y,[y,x]),the map h is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1}$$\end{document}C1. Furthermore, there exists a nonempty open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U\subset W^u_F(x)$$\end{document}U⊂WFu(x) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h|_U$$\end{document}h|U is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C ^{1}$$\end{document}C1 diffeomorphism. For the proofs of the properties of the map h see Proposition 3.6 and Corollary 4.7 in [7].

The next result will be of great help in proving the decay of correlations for the geometrical neutral Lorenz flow. The original statement involves the geometrical Lorenz flow, but the same arguments can be used to prove the same result for our setting. Before stating the result we give a definition.

Definition 3.11

Let X and F be as in the beginning of Section 6.3. A subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_0\subset X$$\end{document}Z0⊂X is called a finite subsystemof X if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_0=\bigcap _{n\ge 0}F^{-n}Z$$\end{document}Z0=⋂n≥0F-nZ, where Z is the union of finitely many partition elements of X.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_1$$\end{document}Q1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_2\in \mathcal {Q}$$\end{document}Q2∈Q be two partition elements and consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=Q_1\cup Q_2$$\end{document}Q=Q1∪Q2. We define the finite subsystem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_0=\bigcap _{n\ge 0}F^{-n}Q$$\end{document}Q0=⋂n≥0F-nQ, then we have the following:

Proposition 3.12

[7, Proposition 3.8] For the finite subsystem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_0$$\end{document}Q0, the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T(Q_0\times Q_0)$$\end{document}T(Q0×Q0) has positive lower box dimension.

We will like to end this Section with the following remark. To establish their results on decay of correlations, Bálint et al. in [12] and Melbourne in [30] assumed a very important, yet technical property namely, absence of approximate eigenfunctions. They also provide some criteria that guarantees the absence of approximate eigenfunctions. The first one, involves the temporal distortion function providing a nonintegrability condition. This criteria is given by Proposition 3.12; that is, when the temporal distortion function is not identically zero. In other words, when the UNI condition is satisfied. The second one, is a Diophantine condition on the periods of three periodic solutions [21], which is satisfied with probability one. It is important to remark that from these criteria, the UNI condition is robust while the Diophantine condition is not.

Decay of Correlations

In this section we prove Theorem 1.1 for the first two neutral models. The third and more general model will be analysed in Sect. 5. We will use the results of Bálint et al. [12] to prove our theorem. In [12] polynomial decay of correlations for non-uniformly hyperbolic flows is proven under absence of approximate eigenfunctions. Let us start by giving the description of a non-uniformly hyperbolic flow described in [12].

First, we observe that the geometrical neutral Lorenz flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {N}}^t : \Lambda _{{\text {Neu}}} \rightarrow \Lambda _{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}Nt:ΛNeu→ΛNeu, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}ΛNeu is the geometrical neutral Lorenz attractor, can be modelled as the suspension flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^t : \Sigma ^{r_{{\text {Neu}}}} \rightarrow \Sigma ^{r_{{\text {Neu}}}}$$\end{document}St:ΣrNeu→ΣrNeu over the Poincaré map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}PNeu with base the cross-section \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma $$\end{document}Σ and roof function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}rNeu from (6). However, in order to use the results of [12], we take the alternative model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t : X^R \rightarrow X^R$$\end{document}Ft:XR→XR, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\subset \Sigma $$\end{document}X⊂Σ is a cross-section to the flow with nice hyperbolic structure (local product structure) and with induced roof function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R : X \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}R:X→R+ given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(x)=\sum _{k=0}^{\tau (x)-1}r_{{\text {Neu}}}(P_{{\text {Neu}}}^k(x))$$\end{document}R(x)=∑k=0τ(x)-1rNeu(PNeuk(x)), see Sect. 3.2. Then the suspension flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t$$\end{document}Ft built over the uniformly hyperbolic map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F : X \rightarrow X$$\end{document}F:X→X is identical to the suspension flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_t$$\end{document}St, thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_t$$\end{document}Ft is an extension of the underlying flow, namely the neutral geometrical Lorenz flow. Within this framework, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {N}}^t$$\end{document}Nt is called in [12] a non-uniformly hyperbolic flow.

Under suitable conditions it can be shown that the suspension flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t$$\end{document}Ft described above is a Gibbs-Markov flow [12, Section 6]. Therefore, the mixing rates for non-uniformly hyperbolic flows can be deduced from the corresponding results for Gibbs-Markov flows, see [12, Corollary 8.1].

For observables v and w, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _t(v,w)$$\end{document}ρt(v,w) denote the decay of correlations of the geometrical neutral Lorenz flow; that is,32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho _t(v,w)=\Bigg |\int v\cdot w\circ N^td\mu -\int vd\mu \int wd\mu \Bigg |, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρt(v,w)=|∫v·w∘Ntdμ-∫vdμ∫wdμ|,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ is the SRB measure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {N}}^t$$\end{document}Nt. Before giving the proof of Theorem 1.1 for the neutral models 1 and 2, we will give the definitions of the space of observables.

Let (M, d) be a metric space with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {diam}}(M)\le 1$$\end{document}diam(M)≤1 and define a flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^t : M \rightarrow M$$\end{document}Tt:M→M on M.
