We fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in (0,1]$$\end{document}η∈(0,1] and for a given observable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v : M \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}v:M→R we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |v|_{C^\eta }=\sup _{x\ne y}\frac{|v(x)-v(y)|}{d(x,y)^\eta }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|v|Cη=supx≠y|v(x)-v(y)|d(x,y)η,and the norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\Vert v\right\Vert _{{C^\eta }}=|v|_\infty +|v|_{C^\eta }$$\end{document}vCη=|v|∞+|v|Cη. We define the Banach space of Hölder functions on M by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\eta (M)$$\end{document}Cη(M); i.e.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^\eta (M)=\{v : M \rightarrow \mathbb {R}\;|\;\left\Vert v\right\Vert _{{C^\eta }}<\infty \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cη(M)={v:M→R|vCη<∞}.Furthermore, let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |v|_{C^{0,\eta }}=\sup _{\begin{array}{c} x\in M\\ t>0 \end{array}}\frac{|v(T^t(x))-v(x)|}{t^\eta } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|v|C0,η=supx∈Mt>0|v(Tt(x))-v(x)|tηand define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\Vert v\right\Vert _{{C^{0,\eta }}}=|v|_\infty +|v|_{C^{0,\eta }}$$\end{document}vC0,η=|v|∞+|v|C0,η. We denote the space of Hölder observables in the flow direction by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^{0,\eta }(M)=\{v : M \rightarrow \mathbb {R}\;|\;\left\Vert v\right\Vert _{{C^{0,\eta }}}<\infty \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C0,η(M)={v:M→R|vC0,η<∞}.We will say that an observable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w : M \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}w:M→R is differentiable in the flow direction if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _tw=\lim _{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{w\circ T^t-w}{t} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tw=limt→0w∘Tt-wtexists pointwise. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\Vert w\right\Vert _{{C^{m,\eta }}}=\sum _{k=0}^m\left\Vert \partial _t^kw\right\Vert _{{C^\eta }}$$\end{document}wCm,η=∑k=0m∂tkwCη. We will denote the space of observables that are m-times differentiable in the flow direction by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^{m,\eta }(M)=\{w : M \rightarrow \mathbb {R}\;|\;\left\Vert w\right\Vert _{{C^{m,\eta }}}<\infty \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cm,η(M)={w:M→R|wCm,η<∞}.For a Borel set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\subset M$$\end{document}X⊂M we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\eta (X)$$\end{document}Cη(X) as above by using the restriction of the metric d to X.

Proof Theorem 1.1

We first note that the suspension flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t$$\end{document}Ft projects to a quotient suspension semiflow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}}^t : {\tilde{X}}^R \rightarrow {\tilde{X}}^R$$\end{document}F~t:X~R→X~R, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{X}}$$\end{document}X~ is the quotient space obtained from X after quotienting out the stable leaves and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}} : {\tilde{X}} \rightarrow {\tilde{X}}$$\end{document}F~:X~→X~ is a Gibbs-Markov map. Proposition 3.9 ensures that the following inequality holds.33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\varphi (x)-\varphi (y)|\le C\gamma ^{s(x,y)}\inf _{Q_i}\varphi \quad \text {for all }x,\; y\in {\tilde{Q}}_i,\; i\ge 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|φ(x)-φ(y)|≤Cγs(x,y)infQiφfor allx,y∈Q~i,i≥1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\tilde{Q}}_i\}_{i\ge 0}$$\end{document}{Q~i}i≥0 is a countable Lebesgue modulo zero partition into subintervals, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\gamma <1$$\end{document}0<γ<1 and s(x, y) is the separation time.

Therefore, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{F}}^t$$\end{document}F~t is a Gibbs-Markov semiflow and consequently that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t$$\end{document}Ft is a Gibbs-Markov flow. Then the conclusion follows from [12, Corollary 8.1].

There are still four details concerning the hypothesis in [12, Corollary 8.1] that we have not mentioned yet. The first one is regarding condition (H); for us this condition holds automatically since R is constant along stable leaves.

The second concerns the absence of approximate eigenfunctions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t$$\end{document}Ft. Melbourne gave in [30, Chapter 5] sufficient conditions for the absence of approximate eigenfunctions, namely the existence of a finite subsystem with positive lower box dimension. Hence, it follows from Proposition 3.12 and Lemma 8.9 in [12] that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t$$\end{document}Ft has absence of approximate eigenfunctions.

The third concerns the tails of R; that is, we want to estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(R>t)$$\end{document}μX(R>t). From Observation 3.5 we know that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(\tau >n)$$\end{document}μX(τ>n) has exponential tails; i.e., there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0>0$$\end{document}c0>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(\tau >n)=\mathcal {O}({\text {e}}^{-c_0n})$$\end{document}μX(τ>n)=O(e-c0n). Moreover, by Theorem 2.1 we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(r_{{\text {Neu}}}>t)$$\end{document}μX(rNeu>t) has polynomial tails; that is, there is a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_1>0$$\end{document}c1>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(r_{{\text {Neu}}}>t)=\mathcal {O}(c_1t^{-\beta _2})$$\end{document}μX(rNeu>t)=O(c1t-β2), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _2=\frac{a_2+b_2}{2b_2}$$\end{document}β2=a2+b22b2. Then by [18, Proposition 5.1] we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(R>t)=\mathcal {O}((\ln t)^{\beta _2}t^{-\beta _2})$$\end{document}μX(R>t)=O((lnt)β2t-β2).

The fourth and last detail concerns how to improve the estimates for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(R>t)$$\end{document}μX(R>t) and remove the logarithmic term. For this, we make use of [11, Lemma 4.1]. There the settings is made for infinite horizon planar periodic Lorentz gases, for that setting the tails of the flow time (in this work denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}rNeu) is of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(t^{-2})$$\end{document}O(t-2). By replacing in [11, Lemma 4.1] the order of the tails from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(t^{-2})$$\end{document}O(t-2) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(c_1t^{-\beta _2})$$\end{document}O(c1t-β2) we can use the same proof to remove the logarithmic term. Hence, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _X(R>t)=\mathcal {O}(t^{-\beta _2})$$\end{document}μX(R>t)=O(t-β2). With this we conclude our proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The natural questions is about the lower bounds, i.e., if the bounds given in this theorem are sharp.

Although we definitely think they are, the currently available literature is insufficient to conclude this, although the margin is fairly narrow.

In [24], the renewal operator methods are developed to get such lower bounds, but his paper is for maps, not flows. Melbourne and Terhesiu come the closest in [31], where they consider suspension semiflows with polynomial roof functions over Gibbs-Markov base maps, and indeed, their results imply polynomial mixing for the flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t : X^R \rightarrow X^R$$\end{document}Ft:XR→XR, for Hölder observables that are constant on the stable fibers. The step from this suspension flow to the actual flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {N}}^t : \Lambda _{{\text {Neu}}} \rightarrow \Lambda _{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}Nt:ΛNeu→ΛNeu, however, is not trivial at all. (Here the effect of the second inducing step needs to be undone, in a way).

In [18] this step was taken for discrete suspensions (i.e., Young towers), specifically for billiard maps, but not for flows. However, the lower bounds that we obtain for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^t : X^R \rightarrow X^R$$\end{document}Ft:XR→XR as a corollary of the results in [31] are sufficient to prove stable laws (with exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/\beta _2 \in (1,2]$$\end{document}1/β2∈(1,2]) for the neutral Lorenz flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {N}}^t : \Lambda _{{\text {Neu}}} \rightarrow \Lambda _{{\text {Neu}}}$$\end{document}Nt:ΛNeu→ΛNeu, cf. [15, 19].

Numerical Analysis and Results

In this section we provide the results of the numerical approximation we obtained for the exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β of the Dulac map (see Equation (8)) and the exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _2$$\end{document}β2 of the tails of the return map (see Theorem 2.1) for the two-dimensional setting (the setting of [17]) and for the 3-dimensional setting concerning this work.

Numerics of the 2-Dimensional Case

To verify the existing theoretical asymptotics from [17], we will start the numerical analysis in the 2-dimensional case; that is, we consider the framework of [15] and [17]. There, the following neutral form was considered:34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \begin{array}{ccc} {\dot{x}}\\ {\dot{y}}\\ \end{array}\right) = \left( \begin{array}{ccc} x(a_0x^\kappa + a_2z^\kappa )\\ -y(b_0x^\kappa + b_2y^\kappa )\\ \end{array}\right) +\mathcal {O}(4), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x˙y˙=x(a0xκ+a2zκ)-y(b0xκ+b2yκ)+O(4),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0,\;a_2,\;b_0$$\end{document}a0,a2,b0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_2>0$$\end{document}b2>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta :=a_2b_0-a_0b_2\ne 0$$\end{document}Δ:=a2b0-a0b2≠0. For simplicity we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =2$$\end{document}κ=2. For the analysis of the Dulac map close to the neutral equilibrium of Equation (34), we take an unstable leaf \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^u(0,y_0)$$\end{document}Wu(0,y0) and a stable leaf \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^s(x_0,0)$$\end{document}Ws(x0,0), then the Dulac map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D : W^u(0,y_0) \rightarrow W^s(x_0,0)$$\end{document}D:Wu(0,y0)→Ws(x0,0), shown in Fig. 3, assigns the firs intersection of the integral curve through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y_0)$$\end{document}(x,y0) with the stable leaf \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^s(x_0,0)$$\end{document}Ws(x0,0), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in W^u(0,y_0)$$\end{document}x∈Wu(0,y0) and T is the flow time; that is, the exit time.

For the setting considered in this work, we will perform the numerical experiments with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0=1$$\end{document}x0=1. In order to corroborate the estimates of the Dulac map given by Equation (8), we expect the numerical experiments to show us that35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta \approx \frac{\ln (y)}{\ln (x)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β≈ln(y)ln(x).We actually will see that36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta =\frac{\ln (y)}{\ln (x)}-\frac{\ln (c(y_0))}{\ln (x)}+\mathcal {O}\Big (\frac{1}{\ln (x)}\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β=ln(y)ln(x)-ln(c(y0))ln(x)+O(1ln(x)).From [15] and [17] we know that the constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(y_0)$$\end{document}c(y0) are given by a specific formula which is not easy to compute. For this reason, we decided to use the least-squares method to calculate these constants.

For the numerical experiments we will take different values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β and 250 points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in [1.0 \times 10^{-5},1.0 \times 10^{-4}]$$\end{document}x∈[1.0×10-5,1.0×10-4] at the unstable leave \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^u(0,y_0)$$\end{document}Wu(0,y0) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_0=1.0$$\end{document}y0=1.0. The integration method we will use for the numerical experiments concerning this work is the so-called Radau quadrature method, to deal with the numerical complications of integrating near a neutral stationary point, see [26].

Figure 8a), b) show us the approximation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β (the red graph), the adjusted approximation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β (green graph), and the theoretical value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β (blue graph) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =0.266$$\end{document}β=0.266 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =0.40$$\end{document}β=0.40, respectively. The approximation of beta is done by taking the last y value of each integral curve and divide it by the x value ranging in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1.0 \times 10^{-5},1.0 \times 10^{-4}]$$\end{document}[1.0×10-5,1.0×10-4], the adjusted beta is calculated using Equation (36). The points-axis corresponds to the 250 x values we considered starting from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1.0 \times 10^{-4}$$\end{document}1.0×10-4 and ending in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1.0 \times 10^{-5}$$\end{document}1.0×10-5; that is, point 0 corresponds to the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=1.0 \times 10^{-4}$$\end{document}x=1.0×10-4 and point 250 corresponds to the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=1.0 \times 10^{-5}$$\end{document}x=1.0×10-5. The constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(y_0)=\ln (0.8)$$\end{document}c(y0)=ln(0.8) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(y_0)=1.2\ln (1.1)$$\end{document}c(y0)=1.2ln(1.1) for the adjusted approximation correspond to Fig. 8a) and b), respectively. The approximations show an error that tends to decrease as we get closer to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=0$$\end{document}x=0 as depicted in the graphs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0=15.0$$\end{document}a0=15.0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Numerics of the 3-Dimensional Case

In this subsection we will perform the numeric experiments for the 3-dimensional models. We will start with Neutral model 1. Note that the strong stable direction of (39) is still purely y-directed.

Until now we have performed the numerical experiments for the Neutral model 1 and 2 corresponding to the normal forms given by Equations (39) and (42), respectively. We saw there that for both models, the asymptotic behaviour of the x and z components are the same as the x and y component of the 2-dimensional model. Recall that for this two models we had explicit formulas for the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N : \Sigma \rightarrow S$$\end{document}N:Σ→S and hence for the modified Poincaré map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{{\text {Neu}}} : \Sigma \rightarrow \Sigma $$\end{document}PNeu:Σ→Σ. Our goal is to see whether the asymptotic behaviour of the Neutral model 3 given by Equation (4) is similar to the other two models. Therefore, the numerical results obtained for the Neutral models 1 and 2 will be our reference and we will compare them to the numerical results obtained for the third model.

In the previous subsection we saw the numerical analysis on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 : \Sigma \rightarrow S$$\end{document}N1:Σ→S and showed, with the numeric experimentation, that the x and z components behaves like the 2-dimensional model. For the next numerical analysis, we will take an unstable leaf \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^u(x,y_0,z_0)$$\end{document}Wu(x,y0,z0) and a stable leaf \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^s(1,0,0)$$\end{document}Ws(1,0,0), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_0=1.0$$\end{document}y0=1.0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0=1.0$$\end{document}z0=1.0. From the estimates obtained in [17] we can see that43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta _2\approx -\frac{\ln (x)}{\ln (t)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β2≈-ln(x)ln(t),where t is the flow time that it takes a point from the unstable leaf to hit the stable leaf and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in [1.0 \times 10^{-5},1.0 \times 10^{-4}]$$\end{document}x∈[1.0×10-5,1.0×10-4]. This numerical experiment has shown us a good approximation of the exponent of the decay of correlations for the 3 neutral models. From this we can deduce the same results concerning the decay of correlations, and obtaining Theorem 1.1 in its full generality.
